And all the more, we're on the agenda with the legislative texts. Comprehensive discussion of the draft law regulating external action and cooperation for the development of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u2	Amendments have been tabled to the entire Socialist Parliamentary Group and to the Parliamentary Group of the Galician Nationalist Block. For the presentation of the project, the Vice-President, Prime Minister, Justice and Tourism, Mr. Alfonso Rueda Valenzuela, has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u3	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Good morning, gentlemen. In fact, the Council of Galicia brings to Parliament the draft law of external action and cooperation for the development of Galicia, a law made for our autonomous community and which aims to establish an updated and comprehensive regulation in the field of external relations. So today we have fulfilled a commitment that was ambitious from the beginning, that it was to equip Galicia with a pioneering standard for many aspects and that it aims to improve our projection through the coordination of all the Greek external action — of course, the one that makes the self-government itself but also all those agents acting abroad. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, this normative text is the fruit of a work and a government commitment that has been working on for years. It should be recalled that in 2014 the adoption of Law 2/2014, of 25 March, of the action and of the State's external service, which was subsequently followed by Law 25/2014, of 27 November, of treaties and other international agreements, opened the doors for the first time to the external projection of autonomous communities. So, with this endorsement and within ever - and I want to insist very much because it is one of the axes, probably the fundamental, it could not be otherwise, which presides over this bill -..., within ever, as I said, of the indispensable and indispensable constitutional loyalty, the Council of Galicia has already approved in 2015 Decree 178 of 26 November, which regulates the external action of the Autonomous Community of Galicia. And then, in 2017, on 9 March, we also adopted the Development Cooperation Decree, which sought to simplify and update the development legislation of a law which had already been old-fashioned for the passage of time, the 2003 Law, of 19 June, of development cooperation. So, and with these background, the Galician Union, in character prior to the adoption of this bill, already on February 1, 2018 approved the Greek Strategy of External Action Horizon post-2020. He had no vote against the report presented in respect of this strategy in the Galician Parliament. And, as you know, point 33 of this strategy reflects the commitment, which we today have fulfilled, to develop and bring to its approval a bill of external action. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we are faced with an important law that responds to the new challenges — dynamic and changing rights — that are always given in the international context. External action is vital in an increasingly interdependent world, increasingly globalized, more and more communicated. Therefore, it is increasingly likely that it will affect every thing that happens in the rest of the countries, in the autonomous communities and in the actors that act in the international context. We therefore have to wager and regulate by law responsible and supportive action — and the law also devotes an important part — to cooperation with the least developed countries. As you know, there is only one precedent, the Foreign Act of Action of Catalonia. And as you also know, it had to be thoroughly revised after facing serious constitutional conflicts with the Central Government. It is therefore perfectly understandable that, with these precedents, and bearing in mind that it is a law of pioneering external action and that it presents a lot of news but a law that is presented in an autonomous community that is part of a State, this constitutional loyalty leads us not to repeat — or at least try not to repeat — the errors that have led to all the problems that have led to that law of external action in Catalonia — even from a constitutional point of view. So we want to do things well, develop a law of consensus, develop a law with loyalty and, therefore, we have dialogue and count from the outset with the participation of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. And, of course, after a negotiation there was a long negotiation from a political point of view, from a technical point of view, we finally count on your good visa, with a good view of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, of the European Union and of Cooperation. So this bill, which we are presenting today, respects the principles of unity of action abroad and institutional loyalty, which must guide — so said the law passed by the State five years ago — the external action of any autonomous community that wants to undertake it and regulate it. Therefore, Galicia's external action is presided over — and I would like to stress very much — by constitutional loyalty. Without constitutional loyalty, in external action — not at all, ladies and gentlemen, but especially in external action — there is no cooperation or coordination with the central government or even the external action we want to do. The project went through all the processes of public participation, achieved the favourable report of the Galego External Action Council and the Galego Development Cooperation Council — this last report was unanimously, I think it is important to highlight it. And, therefore, with these background, and before I get a little bit more into its contents, I'd like to highlight in general lines that are a pioneering standard. It is the second which is done — first in coordination — in cooperation with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the favourable report; that is, the State itself, which has, at the end, not forgotten — exclusive competence in the field of international relations. And we aspire with it to improve Galician projection through the coordination of our external action. We intend to regulate the Greek participation in the negotiation of international treaties — and this is also very important, much more so now that a new economic period is opened in Europe —. We want to improve the attention of the Greeks abroad, consolidate the very important relationship — which has come for many years — of the northern Galicia region of Portugal. We want to project Galicia's action in the countries of the Lusophony and provide Galicia with the greatest possible role in development cooperation. And, of course, with regard to Galicia's external image, external action on tourism will also focus on the Galician brand and, at the gates of the Xacobeo 2021, especially on the Way of Santiago. Entering into detail the objectives of the law, what do we intend with this law? He said before that of proposing responses to the new and changing challenges that arise in the international context and getting strengthened with these proposals. We sought, among other things, to take advantage of the opportunities, which I had previously referred to, which opened Law 2/2014 of 25 March, of the action of the State's external service and also Law 25/2014 of treaties and other international agreements. They give us opportunities to take advantage of them by developing a law in the autonomic field, and that's why we do it and introduce it. We also want to project Galicia into the European Union and the rest of the international scene — much more at the moment. We also want to improve the coordination of the public actors and private Greek agents who operate abroad. We want to assemble, renew and make sense — and to continue to be dynamic — to the cooperation of Galicia in external action for development. I believe that the experience of more than three highlights in the management of development cooperation policy now allows — listening to the NGOs, those on the ground — to incorporate more appropriate solutions, with a management that has to continue to earn — and it already has a lot — in efficiency for beneficiaries and initiatives, which are always carried out in very complicated and always needed scenarios of much coordination and great precision in the support given to them. Let us turn to the concrete measures contained in this law, how we are going to do all of this to what I'm talking about. Thanks to the fact that we are going to mention — and I hope — the general framework needed by this law will allow us to do many things in the concrete plane, we will be able to determine the guiding principles of the Greek external action; for example, we will be able to contribute to real convergence with the European Union's socio-economic indicators through our own external action; we will be able to continue to act in the diversification of the external markets; we will be able to act more directly in the collection of commercial and industrial capital for productive investment, and also of qualified human capital — which is very important — for the economic consolidation of Galicia; we will work to promote the return of immigrants and their descendants who want to do so — always have the doors of Galicia opened and this law will be an important instrument; and we will also deepen — which is very important — in the implementation of the objectives of the United Nations's global development agenda. The law also allows the concentration in the figure of the Presidency of the Galician Council of the external representation of our autonomous community and also gives it the ability to subscribe to international agreements as far as the Autonomous Community of Galicia is concerned. The subjects of external action, both public and private, are also much more concrete, giving special importance to those acting in the territorial sphere of the euro region. It provides for greater transparency in official travel abroad, in line with the Greek external action strategy, which already provides for it at its own time, and with that annual report which is brought to Parliament. We also want to regulate and inform — this is very important and, in addition, it was one of the demands requested by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs — all receipts and visits made by foreign agents and agents — of any kind, public and private — to come to Galicia, and to bring a relationship and coordination with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. It is recognized, as I said before, the strategic character of the Galicia-North region of Portugal and it is also very important all that has to do with the Lusophony. Cooperation with Portugal, with the Lusophone countries is becoming more and more closely, and we must therefore take advantage of this enormous advantage. We also address issues related to Galicia's participation in European Union affairs through the Conference on European Union Affairs (CAREU). We see it's an instrument not yet exploited. It needs to have a lot more capacity, it needs to greatly improve the coordination of autonomous communities with the General Administration of the State through CARUE. We believe that it is the right instrument and we also want to regulate in this law the way in which we believe that Galicia should participate, in an ascending and descending sense, in the drafting of the agreements that are taken to CARUE for coordination purposes. We also regulate participation in the sectoral conferences that have to do with foreign policy, such as the Working Party on European Affairs and External Action or the European Committee of the Regions — which is fundamental and where Galicia has played a special role in recent years. All of this is provided for in the External Act of Action. Galicia's participation in Europe outside the legal framework of the European Union is of particular importance. Why? Yes, of course, for the present moment we live and for all the difficulties that are being posed by the Brexit-derived agreements. We therefore want to be able to regulate the negotiation of treaties and their execution in Galicia outside the framework of the European Union, where appropriate, and international administrative and non-regulatory agreements, provision for other agreements, new figures that may suggest, communicate and register these instruments, coordination again with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and also the approval of memorandums, which are increasingly a legal instrument which has more use in the field of international relations. It's a law, in addition, which has a deep cross-sectional vocation; therefore, it regulates all the departments of the Galician Union and systematizes them for their external action. In the area of emigration, for example, we are going to look after the rights of Greek citizenship abroad and to facilitate — as I said before — their return to Galicia at a time when we believe that achieving this goal is especially important. As far as emergency matters are concerned, we're going to coordinate even more. We've started with the Ariem projects in northern Portugal, but we think there's a lot more field of development. First, to be much more agile — and, the less bureaucratic, better — in response to emergencies that have a border character; and also, when international aid is required — even if it is not in close quarters — that the system works very quickly, that it is not lost a single minute when such international aid is called for. Galicia can send it out, and on the contrary — and we hope that it will never be necessary — when it is needed here, in Galicia, that international aid from other countries and that there is a procedure that will facilitate it in an axile way. At the Galaga School of Public Administration there will also be a training system for public employees in the field of external relations and of European and international interest. Of course, at the expense of all economic planning you have to have — you have, but you have to continue to have, and much more now that it is a new economic period — a very related and highly connected perspective with the management of European funds. In the environment we will bet on external environmental cooperation. In economic terms, in the economic relations between Galician companies and Galician companies, we will prioritize all those who have an external vocation. There are many and very good ones, which I think are examples throughout Spain and throughout the world of vocation to look outward and to export; therefore, you will have to continue to potentiate them. They are located here in Galicia and, of course, they are an instrument that we must take advantage of. So in the first trimester of each year, the Autonomous Administration will evaluate the World Bank's Doing Business Study, present for this, to assess and launch measures to further improve this foreign capital attraction and to continue to assist companies that look abroad in their commercial and industrial activity. In education we will encourage European exchanges of teaching professionals and students. We're already having a successful program with northern Portugal, the James Program, and we want to keep doing it. There are many more possibilities to keep growing in this field. We will take precedence over the internationalization of the universities in Greece in university funding. We will continue to participate in the European Union’s more and more powerful social volunteering programmes. And in culture and language we will continue to promote the dissemination of cultural values and the external visibility of the Galician cultural fabric, the teaching of the language and the literature of Welsh, and to take advantage of, of course — as I said before — all the potentials that give us the Lusophony. In tourism we will offer a highly oriented Galician brand image, as I said before — and much more at this time — about what has to do with the values of the Santiago Way, which is the most distinctive fact and what recognizes us internationally. But that's not all. We believe that we have many things to offer as brand-Galicia, as country-marks, as tourist destination too; and therefore coordination in external promotion also provides for this law, which will greatly facilitate things. In health care we will promote the appeal of health services for their service in Galicia, just as other countries of the European Union are doing. With regard to the sea and fishing — which is so important in Galicia — there is a specific paragraph to defend the interests of the sea abroad, an active policy of EU agreements with non-member third countries to supply us with products from the sea and to stimulate agreements with non-member States and the European Union to access their waters. In cooperation, I've already told you to update your goals. We believe that we are frankly pleased with the way the NGOs work, with the support given to them from the Galician Union to make their policy on the ground. And that all of this is recognized and coordinated in an outer action law will undoubtedly help you in a very direct way. We have also — and I will finish, Mr President — updated the report on public delegations and offices abroad. We adapt their forecasts to the Law of 2014, of action and of the State's foreign service, and, without overlaping with it at all, we want to continue to maintain, in loyal coordination and collaboration with the Government of the State and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Greek presence in those places, through an office, where the Greeks demand that this presence be more manifest and lasting. We constituted the Seat of Greeks abroad to strengthen links with Galicia, identify the Greek companies abroad, and create networks through the Seat of companies and professional Greeks abroad, and regulate the Voluntary Register of Foreigners with Interests in Galicia, with special link and affection to Galicia, so that here, within Galicia, they lobby out, towards their countries, of all that comes here, and that they help us also to be ambassadors in their places of origin of all that they have experienced here and everything they have perceived here. So, finally, ladies and gentlemen, we have fulfilled a commitment demanded by many sectors of society, we take advantage of the opportunity given to us by the laws of the State to make an external law of action in Galicia. We have very clear which constitutional frameworks we should not move from, but we also have clear that, within these frameworks, if we can do a lot of things and we should not waste this opportunity. It's a law of an autonomous community that aspires to have its own role abroad, but it's not a law of a state. The state is the Spanish State and therefore...
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u4	Finish, please.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u5	...we want to make that coordination, we want to continue to exercise that constitutional loyalty. We do not want to fall into the mistakes of the other law of external action that existed right now in the autonomous community, and we want to be pioneers at a time when we take the lead in international relations, no doubt — if we do it right and for that it is essential a law — will give Galicia many advantages in the future. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u7	Thank you, Mr. President. We are faced with a text that seeks to legislate on important issues for Galicia, given its special external projection within the autonomous communities of the State for basically historical reasons, as is the fact that, according to certain estimates, 1 out of every 5 European emigrants — who passed from 60 million between 1850 and 1950 — was Greek or Greek. The singularity that this Welsh diaspora represents and the fact that we find ourselves in an increasingly globalized and interdependent world require, of course, a very fine adjustment to the policies of our community within the scope of its autonomy. But we can't lose sight that in any federal state — and Spain is, at least to a good extent, a federal state — the normal thing is that foreign policies are part of the sphere of action of the central structure of the state, for logical and well-founded reasons of efficiency and coherence; so the centrifugal temptation that may have some political force in this House does not make sense of any kind, neither from the point of view of the structural rationality of the state nor from the point of view of the trends of the world in the 21st century. What makes all the sense is to provide Galicia, within the rationality and legal order, with effective and efficient external action; efficiency and efficiency of which it has traditionally been lacking — solely in the last eleven years. The Greek diaspora, which we all know, was a tragic story, to a good extent, that of a country from which the people had to emigrate to survive. But within this tragedy, the diaspora was also enriching by the fact that it reached a point where it could be said that there was a Welshman in every corner of the world, from Australia to Canada, passing through South Africa or the Amazon. And that prints a character, brand culture and equips this country with an asset underutilized by the managerial incapacity in the matter of the Greek Government and the inability of that very government to imagine ways to put this huge foreign Galician community online, which implies a great cultural but also economic potential if Galicia is able to take advantage of it. And that doesn't work out with this law, nor does it look like it's on its way. It's very good to create records and censuses of companies and residents abroad, which is what a decimonic administration would do. But in the 21st century this law goes from points on to how to network all that potential and take advantage of the synergies of Galician foreign capital — which is much and we reiterate that it is underutilized. Instead, it seems that the most important thing that matters to the Union is clientelism. Discretionary aid and cooperation agreements with the Greek centres — which are too often used to manipulate the external vote — proliferated in the campaign and the pre-campaign, after less than a year ago we knew that the State Court of Auditors, in a random control, opened accounts for the diversion of EUR 300,000, which were theoretically intended to help the Spanish community of Venezuela — which, as we know, is largely Welsh — and which were intended for spry purposes of type and electoral character. And, for further inri, the newspaper El Diario subtitulates an information dated February 20, this year saying: "A high post of the Galician Board appointed by Alberto Núñez Feijóo has drawn up a plan in Venezuela for a public foundation to cover with own funds the 300,000 euro hole detected by the Court of Auditors in the management of Spanish emergency aid in that country during 2015 and 2016, two electoral years." That information would be referred to by Mr. Rodríguez Miranda, who has recently been ratified as Secretary General of Emigration. And the chances that the State Court of Auditors detects a deviation in a portion of that kind are frankly low, because it does not control more than a small part of the public expenditure. So we can infer that this falcatrode — which, by the way, presents obvious criminal indications — is only the tip of the iceberg, which gives us an idea of what the priorities of the Junta, Mr Feijóo and Mr Rodríguez Miranda are about the heirs of our diaspora. In any case, the elaboration of this rule does not show sufficient involvement by interested actors, nor by the Greek external community, while few allegations have been made during the hearing period, relatively few. And we're faced with a very extensive, clumsy rule that abounds in Kafkianally bureaucratic matters and declarations as prolific as empty of content. And it's also inaccurate about the means by which it aims to achieve the multiple and, no doubt, well-intentioned objectives that it marks; some as ethereal as to promote Galicia as by scientific and technological excellence. And allow me to stop here because it's a completely alien target to reality, when Galicia is far from the average investment in R & D+i of the state's whole, which, for its part, is far from the European average. Not to mention that the concept of excellence is linguistically as misleading as legally indeterminate. But, in any case, what we have to consider is that the only place where Galicia can be regarded as a pole of scientific and technological excellence that needs to be promoted abroad is in the warm and warm minds of Mr Feijóo's propagandists. But in no case should anything like this be done in a law, because Galicia is far from being excellent in scientific and technological development, for much propaganda to put us in the loophole. First of all, despite receiving large amounts from European funds, Galicia fell down by 27 positions from 2013 to 2019 at the regional competitiveness ranking that the European Union is developing. And of course, we know that competitiveness is closely linked to science and technology. But at this rate and with this government, the possibilities of Galicia becoming, as the law says, a pole of scientific and technological excellence are as high as those of which it becomes the Country of Wonders so that Alice of Lewis Carroll developed with certain looseness. So to say today that Galicia needs to be promoted abroad as well as by scientific and technological excellence is a nonsense, a dream, a ton of hair or all three things at the same time. And putting it in writing in a law is an exercise of political and legislative folly. And instead of deliriums, it doesn't just contemplate a coherent regulation of something as important as the action of economic promotion abroad, beyond obvious objectives, as attracting foreign investment; very commendable if it wasn't, firstly, because Galicia is systematically, year after year, a community that receives much less direct investment than the average of the state. For example, in the last year, 2019, which was announced by the Galician Board as a great success in investment capture, captured 112 million of the 14,792 state, of the state as a whole — that is, less than 1% of the total, when its weight in GDP and in the population of the state as a whole exceeds 5% —. And in 2018, a much more standard year, much more regular, captured 0.14%, i.e. 35 times less than it would correspond to its economic and population weight. They are absolutely demolitional figures that deny Galicia's attractive investor and the ease to do business in it, unlike northern Portugal, that captures the Greek capital and foreign to moreas the Union looks elsewhere and disproves itself from that fact. Secondly, because the means to make Galicia a more competitive country in the international agenda are not contemplated in this law, beyond the delusional idea of examining each year, as Mr Rueda mentioned, the World Bank's Doing Business Report. And in this respect I want to stop especially. It is unusual that a law establishes the obligation for someone in the Greek Government to read such an important report as the Doing Business, in addition to that it seems to imply that until now no one in the Union has bothered to read that report, which seems to confirm what we already thought. But it is unheard of for a number of reasons. First, that report is translated into Spanish, so even Mr. Feijóo, who doesn't speak or understand English, can read it perfectly. Secondly, it's not a very extensive report -- about 130 pages -- so it won't take them long. Thirdly, if you don't have time for Mr. Feijóo or Mr. Rueda or Mr. Count, you have lots of advisers who can give you an executive summary. And here the problem is all over again. Igape is a disaster both in Galicia's external promotion and in foreign investment capture. So much so, even though it has among its ends the promotion of Galicia's foreign trade and the capture of foreign investment, only one of its departments — four or five — has among its functions those purposes. I mean, about 20 of the approximately 120 Igape workers have, as one of their tasks, Galicia's external economic action. So it seems that the Igape is much more anxious to distribute subsidies among certain related companies than to promote the Greek economy abroad, which is, or should be, one of its main objectives. It is a huge disproportion with respect to the importance of this external economic action in the 21st century, something that Mr. Feijóo's Board seems to have not been found, as the fact that in eleven years of government the only foreign investment pickup action by that self-denominated large manager, which is Mr. Feijóo, was that of the Pemex case, which consisted in that a few months before the elections of 2016 the President of the Board announced a wonderful agreement by which Pemex bought the shipyard Barreras and besides he was going to build in Spain more than 20 ships — which eventually stayed in two — and a logistics center for Europe throughout the outer port of Punta Langosteira, of which I never knew anything at all. And the rest of the story — by the way, for bad — is well known. Another problem of the Greek external action that does not solve this rule is the dispersion in its direction, which, on the one hand, is found in the General Secretariat of Emigration, which depends on Mr. Feijóo, and, on the other, in the General Directorate of External Action, which I believe depends on Mr. Rueda. This is very difficult to coordinate policies in the external environment, in addition to being an anomaly, if we make a comparison with other autonomous communities. Another is to lower the rank of the General Secretariat of External Action of the Fraga era and also of the legislation of Emilio Pérez Touriño. All of the above, and much more, gives evidence of the lack of determination of a law, which seems at times completely alien to reality, which advocates the capture of specialized immigrants abroad when Galicia basically suffers from an economical demographic crisis that presents Spain's worst data and unemployment figures that duplicate those of the OECD before they are triggered by the crisis resulting from the current pandemic. Ladies and gentlemen of the PP, how will Galicia capture specialized immigrants when the best-prepared youth in Galicia's history has to go out of Galicia to find work? It is another abismal incoherence, another abismal incoherence between official Galicia and royal Galicia. In the official Galicia we capture specialized immigrants — yes, on the role — while in the royal Galicia the young men and girls, after spending years of their training with public support and personal and family effort, have to pack up to find a job that is minimally decent. This law dedicates a title, the ninth, to delegations and permanent autonomous offices abroad, in which it seems an attempt to mimic acts of communities such as Catalonia, which have been very controversial in recent years and which seem to be very poorly tuned, insofar as the current crisis is predictable a dilated contraction in the time of available public resources, which in any case are limited and scarce and, as such, frankly valuable. And, on the other hand, it is obvious that there is a wide network of state embassies and commercial offices abroad that can serve — and in fact they have been serving for decades and are, by the way, underutilized — for the purposes of this law without the need to invest scarce Greek public resources in new buildings which are useless. And in general, in all your public policies, you should change your mindset. The Union has dedicated itself for decades to building empty buildings, and Galicia's external action, like the rest of our community's policies, will not be better than having more empty or meaningless buildings and, therefore, that which they like so much, which is "the sweet one." The law dedicates a title, the tenth, to new networks of Greek interests abroad, in a completely imprecise and, with all probability, sterile manner. The Union stands out as a novelty the creation of the Seat of Galicians abroad and more the Seat of Galician companies abroad, as well as the Register of Foreigners with Interests in Galicia, which will voluntarily include those non-Spanish and residents outside Spain who have a special link with Galicia. But besides saying that there will be those records, it doesn't set what you're going to do with them. So we're faced with another Kafkiana derivative of a law that seems to have had the same Kafka on his editorial team. In conclusion — and to conclude — we understand that it is a well-intentioned law that addresses the problems of the Greek external action between immobilism, self-competition and an extremely bureaucratic vision — that it would almost be optimistic to qualify it by the 20th century itself — when we are in the 21st century. Nothing more and thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u26	Mr. President, ladies and gentlemen. "The demographic ageing of the population of Greece is higher than that of the European Economic Community as a whole. This fact is further emphasized when examining the most extensive rural areas of Galicia separately, where generational renewal is at a critical time. Changing this trend is a preferred but difficult-to-achieve goal, because its goals are necessarily achieved in the long term. But if we do not act over time — and now we are — in the near future the situation will be irreversible. We cannot afford an aging Galicia, because this constitutes the main obstacle to the renewal of our human resources and a serious threat to our future." All these words aren't mine. It was announced by President Manuel Fraga Iribarne at the 1992 Autonomy State debate, which was already present in his first investment debate in January 1990. Three decades later this certificate diagnosis still maintains its vigil. The bill we bring to this Parliament today is the culmination of a task: to place the people's issue in the political debate. A task in which Galicia was a pioneer then, since our approach to demographics had no parallelism in any other autonomous community and in which Galicia continues to be a pioneer today, because the Demographic Push Law we are discussing today will be the first one at the national level. Today, when everyone in the Western world speaks of demography in the same terms that were done here thirty years ago, Galicia continues to be at the forefront of raising this issue, for the first time, to a rule of law. It's not an isolated law, but the logical continuation of a demographic work that has attracted special intensity over the past eleven years. The hemerothecas are the efforts of the Galician Union to bring the demographic issue to the national agenda and to the European agenda. We have brought together a number of communities with similar concern and we have given our opinions to the European institutions. And more recently, President Feijóo achieved at the 2017 Conference of Presidents that the Government of Spain had created the High Commissioner for the Demographic Challenge. Returning to the Galician sphere, the consecration of demography at the highest political priorities has had some effects of those that have benefited all the Greek families for years: the Welcome Card, a direct economic aid for the upbringing of young children who have already reached about 60,000 homes, and the children's homes, 81 centres that have been created and created possibilities for reconciliation in the smaller municipalities of Galicia, in the rural municipalities, where there has never been such a service before; the important extension of the public network of children's schools, which has assumed that since 2009 children's care squares have doubled to children aged between 0 and 3 years; and this year 2020 the approval of the total gratuity of child education for the second and successive children, a measure with which, once again, Galicia has become leader at the national level. The Demographic Push Law comes to perform a double function: on the one hand, shielding and consolidating all progress made over these years and, on the other, moving forward towards a demographic policy of consensus and long-term which is useful for Galicia, and not only for the coming legislation, but for the next decades. Throughout its 106 articles and 13 provisions, this law deals with demography in its complex and polyedral character. It's not just a matter of making it easy for families with children or wanting to have them, which too. It's not just a matter of moving forward towards an easier reconciliation and towards a more rational schedule, too. It's not just about giving young people the opportunities they demand and deserve, that they too. It's not just about the uptake of a new population, especially through the promotion of the Greeks from abroad, which too. It's not just about active and healthy aging and promotional -- which also -- nor is it just about the necessary territorial balance and vertebration of Galicia, which, of course, also. The law that we present today addresses all these issues, their interrelationships, the consequences and the repercussions and how they result in such a diffuse context and, at the same time, as broad as the demographic impulse. Of course, it does not go down to very specific aspects, nor does it fall into the concrete regulation of the measures that will be implemented over the next few years. It would be against any legislative technique to seek to establish in a law aspects that are defined more accurately and agilely in a text of lower rank. I also assumed a very negative interference in the agenda of the self-governments that happen to us. This law does not intend to impose unmovable demographic impulse measures, but rather the guiding principles that should guide all these actions. Ladies and gentlemen, the rulers and legislators have a responsibility to our peers, but also to our descendants. Working together to achieve the best law of possible demographic impulse is part of our commitment to the Greeks of the morning. PP. of the Galego Nationalist Block and the Galician Socialists, to the draft law of demographic impulse of Galicia. The voting was as follows: votes in favour, 33; votes against, 40. The result of the vote is negative. These amendments are therefore rejected. Institutional declaration
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u45	The vote is over, but I will now read out an institutional statement which, according to the groups, brings me here, and which is on the occasion of the Children's Day, and which says: "The crisis caused by the pandemic of the Covid-19 is having serious consequences for children. We go through exceptional circumstances that lead to an increase in child poverty and inequalities, an increase in the educational gap and a threat to the physical and mental health of children, children, adolescents and their families. These consequences impact more strongly on those who are in greater vulnerability conditions. That's why, on Universal Children's Day, from the Galician Parliament, we want to add our unanimous voice and remember our commitment to the fulfilment of the Convention on the Rights of the Child and the implementation of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. The current situation is testing our ability to respond as a society, not only in the face of the emergency from the coward-19 but in the face of the challenges of the future, so renewing commitment to the rights of children is inescapable. It's time to reimagine a better world for childhood, when, in addition, we started a new legislation in Galicia. For that, it will be essential that since the public authorities — and especially from our home, Parliament — we renew our commitment to children, children and adolescents, and also to the 2030 Agenda as a collective roadmap to respond to the current emergency and to rebuild better, reimagining a more equitable, sustainable and just world for all. To make that reality, we must ensure that the rights of children and children are at the centre of crisis care and recovery. We must make firm decisions to ensure that no child or child is left behind, and that each one of them can develop its maximum potential. Already prior to the arrival of the coronavirus, poverty and exclusion affected 26% of the child population in Galicia; that is, many families are facing the situation caused by the coward 19 under conditions of extreme vulnerability. That's why we must work together to prevent the pandemic crisis from becoming a lasting crisis for children. That is why we commit ourselves to promoting the following actions that favour the fulfilment of their rights: —Promoting greater and more efficient investment in children, ensuring that the resources needed to meet the challenges mentioned above are allocated. —Promoting the development of the Galician Law on Children and Adolescents as a framework instrument for articulating future commitments with children, children and adolescents in our community. —Promoting the actions necessary to analyse the impact of covid-19 on children and adolescents in Galicia, especially on the most vulnerable, and addressing their solutions to: 1. Strengthen the social protection system to ensure a suitable standard of living for each child or child — sustainable development goal number 1 —. 2. Addressing the challenges of the educational divide, reducing school failure, early abandonment and digital divide — sustainable development goal number 4 — 3. Ensuring special protection measures for asylum-seekers and refugees, as well as unaccompanied migrants — sustainable development goal number 10 — 4. Ensuring the rights of children deprived of a family environment — sustainable development goal number 16 — 5. Prevent, detect and adequately address cases of violence against children — objective of sustainable development number 16 — 6. Protect and promote the health of children, children and adolescents, with special attention to mental health and the rights of children with disabilities — sustainable development objectives numbers 2, 3 and 10—. 7. Strengthen international cooperation for development by fulfilling the commitment, recognized in the Convention on the Rights of the Child, to provide protection for the rights of children and adolescents worldwide — the objective of sustainable development number 17 —. 8. Listening to the concerns and needs of children, children and adolescents, and contemplating them in a cross-cutting way in political action by promoting their participation in designing, along with them and them, the strategies for the recovery of this crisis. 9. Promote the necessary actions to meet the seventeen sustainable development goals and the Paris Agreement, and convert the current crisis into an opportunity to invest in the present and future of childhood, rebuilding better and with a sustainable approach, without losing sight of the threat of the climate crisis and its impact on children. Finally, recognizing the existing problems and the great challenges we face, we reaffirm our commitment to propose actions and achieve results for children in Galicia, but also at the national and global level, and we declare our direct commitment to children to reimagin with them and them a world in which all their rights can be realized." Mr. Counsellor for Health, on his own request, to report on the evolution of the health emergency of the covid-19 in relation to the general forecasts and lines of action of his department
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u46	To formulate this appearance, you have the word of the health adviser, Mr. Julio García Comesaña.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u47	Good morning, gentlemen. I would like to compare myself in this House to the two duties I have with this House. First of all, to provide regular information on the situation of the health emergency of the covid-19, as it has undertaken to do this Government. Secondly, report on my lines of work, which the Department of Health intends to carry out during this legislation. The situation of the pandemic has a decisive influence on the plans and actions that this board will develop over the next few months, and that is why we consider it appropriate to report together on both issues and to submit to the political groups the set of action lines, which are now being deployed in relation to the pandemic and those that continue, strengthen or initiate in relation to other health promotion programs, assistance or health research. With regard to the evolution of the pandemic, the data we're familiar with endorses the strategy that Galicia took and which explained the president of the Union in the past full. Always supported by the Council of the Clinical Committee have taken balanced and surgical measures to control the progress of the pandemic by maintaining an acceptable level of activity. That's why today 69 of the 313 Greek municipalities have a level of restrictions that the Committee calls "The Carballin Model," and which substantially coincides with the measures that have been put into effect in this golden town on October 21st and which have allowed the incidence of the virus to be reduced to a very significant extent. So on October 20th, the city council of Carballin had an accumulated incidence rate of 14 days of 1,240 cases per 100,000 inhabitants. On November 20, when we made the decision to lift these restrictions, the cumulative incidence had fallen to 85.5. In addition, these data showed stability in time, and the situation throughout the region was similar. We are aware that this has required a month of effort for the citizenship of the municipality, for the hospitality and leisure arrangements and, by extension, for the whole economy. But we believe that the result paid the penalty in terms of preventable infections, reduced hospital pressure in the Ourense area and, consequently, saved lives. In a month, we achieved a reduction of 93% of the accumulated rate to fourteen days and we took this council from a very high level to the middle level — exactly in half of what mark the indicators of the coordinated response document agreed by the National Health System Interterritorial Council. A month later, the Carballino left. We know that achieving this goal in all the municipalities that are under restrictions today is complex, but we cannot and must not give up on it. Its main motivation is to ensure our healthcare capacity and to ensure that, in the same way as in the previous wave, all patients in Galicia are treated with all available means and with the greatest effort to preserve their life and health. Let me give you some more detailed information on today's general situation in Galicia: the number of positives detected in the last 14 days has decreased by 19.9 % — 20 % practically —; in the same way, conventional hospitalization has decreased by 8%; and in the case of patients in ICUs — in this case last week — that reduction was almost 5%. By continuing with some general data, today the number of positives we have in Galicia is 8,617, with a reduction on yesterday's 52 cases. We have 96 patients in ICU, which means 1.1% of these 8,617; 456 patients in conventional hospitalization, which means 5.3%; and 8,065 patients in their homes, which means 93.6%. Of course — and we must admit it — we have 1,161 dead in this period, exactly 15 on yesterday. And go ahead with our memory and homage to them and their families. Speaking in relative terms, this occupation of ICU beds that we have right now — that is 96 patients — is 46.07% less than we had in our peak in spring. At that time we reached 178 patients in ICU and, as I said, today we are in 96 — that's 46.07% less —. And in the same way, in conventional hospitalization we are at 51.8% less than that peak — in the month of April in this case — which was 946 at that maximum value. Today, as I say, we're in 456. The percentage of PCR positivity continues to be higher than we would like to; in particular, on yesterday we had a value of 7.32%. And speaking globally, compared to the rest of the state, the incidence accumulated in Galicia right now is 14 days in 276.90, compared to an average value across the state of 374.52; that is, we are 100 points below the average of the Spanish state. This is achieved by this type of measures we previously commented on, which cut the chain of contagion in those municipalities where the incidence is high, but also by early detection measures in those places where this incidence is low and we want to prevent it from rising. Our system of case detection and handling and their close contacts is in constant review. As you know, from May to May the Galician Union launched a coordinated process of tracking and detecting cases involving epidemiological surveillance services, preventive medical services, primary care professionals, the specific contact tracking centre, and the military professionals that the Ministry of Defence has made available to us, and which, of course, we thank. The evolution of the gromos itself, the increase of the professions of the central and the incorporation of new collectives into the process required a series of adjustments in the procedures to try to try a homogeneous treatment of diagnostic tests such as positive cases and their close contacts. In this context, last September 29 reviewed the protocol for care and handling of cases in primary care by clearly delimiting the functions and releasing these professionals from bureaucratic processing of tests, contacts and their follow-up. This sort of order, requested by our professionals, sparked a false controversy over a supposed ban on the request for evidence that was never so, as demonstrated by the passage of time. The Autonomous Community of Galicia made over the last week an average of 7,000 CRPs a day, which comes to some 49,000 this past week — which has nothing to do with my appearance today in this House — of course. This figure corresponds, as has always been the case, to the absolute freedom of primary professionals to request how much evidence they consider appropriate, to the ability of the follow-up plant to process contact requests, to the maximum provision of health care personnel that attends the car shelters installed in all areas of health and to the immense work of the microbiology and laboratory services of our hospitals to process them. In addition to PCRs performed to symptomatic people or to close contacts of positive cases, Junta continues to make population seizures both in specific territories — such as the cases of Ribadavia, Ribeira or this same weekend in the Vigo area — as well as in groups of special risk. We continue with the support of the entire staff of socio-sanitary residences, which enables us to detect the positives early and thus prevents, as far as possible, the emergence of new groms in these sensitive spaces. The first one of these guys located 20 positives and the second one located 26. And it's already been done a third week last week. On the other hand, we continue to increase our diagnostic capacity by adding to all these PCR new antigen tests, which are added to those of antibodies, of which we have been available for a longer time. After acquiring 50,000 of these new tests in September, Galicia began using them in October in emergency services and in five primary centers as a pilot test for three weeks. Subsequently, and as announced by the president fifteen days ago, 300,000 more antigen tests were acquired to extend their use to all primary centers, which have now been distributed. And we'll continue to incorporate new tests. But if all this isn't enough, we have steps to continue to anticipate ourselves. The Clinical Committee evaluated the contingency plan developed by Sergas for acute respiratory pathologies to face a winter that could unite influenza and curfew. This document, with organisational measures, will be available to all of you, since you have requested it as documentation of the study commission. We also have a strategic stock, which currently has coats for more than five thousand days, masks for more than a thousand days, and glasses or protective screens for more than a thousand days. For example, the daily consumption of mascarillas in the public health system in Greece — only in terms of health — exceeds 59,000 units per day; this makes, for example, consumption of more than 1,40,000 mascarillas on 11 November. And if we're talking about non-sterile protective suits, that daily consumption is 14.323 a day, which makes you consume more than 385,000 such suits in one week. In another order, we have already presented to the sectoral table a plan of continence in the field of staff which is scheduled to take place from the next 1st of December 750 health emergency appointments in the nursing category. It'll last for six months, and 150 of these contracts will last for one year. In other categories, it may be the order of 500 or 400 such contracts — the speech of assistants, captors, PSXs, microbiologists or laboratory technicians —. These professionals are made up of more than 1,700 reinforced personnel since the outbreak of the pandemic. Another issue in which it is essential to act in the face of the repercussions of the health crisis is primary care. I've already talked about the protocol adopted to unbureaucratize the work of these professionals, but we will take more action in the short term. We're aware of the discomfort that the difficulties in telephone access are causing in many people, and we've been working hard to put solutions. For example, we strengthen the administrative areas of many health centres, and there are another series of measures that will be launched in the short term. In December, Sergas will launch a new CRM system, which manages the user relationship, to manage the relationship with patients, both doctors and administrative staff. For administrative staff, this system will allow you to collect the list of all lost phone calls to return them as soon as possible. The user can be assured that his unattended call will be recorded and returned, and that he will not have to call for a second time but will be contacted proactively by his health center. It's already in evidence, both in primary and hospital, a video-call system, or video-consultation system, which will improve the quality of non-presental health care. In December this system will be integrated into a new Sergas mobile application that will remove the need to receive the link in order to connect to the video, which will allow you to directly establish that videoconsult through this application. The new Sergas mobile app will also have new features, such as the management of the appointment request in a much more friendly and fast way, the automatic turnout issue at the time the patient reaches the health center and there has a requested quote, and the availability, for example, of the virtual health card within the app itself, which can be used with the same benefits as the physical card in both health centers and pharmacy. So we continue to believe in the advantages of a mixed model that combines telematics care and presence care, and that's why we're going to improve it. But we're aware that presence must increase and that's why we're formulating to the agencies the goal of locating it, at the end of this year, at 40%. And telematic attention has to work properly, from there the technological media measures I just detailed. But reforms in primary care cannot remain in the short term. I have followed very closely the appearances of Doctors Estrella López-Pardo and Susana Aldecoa in the committee of study created in this Parliament on reactivation. In which they asked precisely to reactivate the development of the primary care plan and the document by primary vertebrate care, interrupted by the pandemic. I agree with them that we can no longer postpone the transformation that primary care should live, a transformation that would lead to it being that gateway from which we are now talking to a central care level with its own resolution capacity. To achieve this objective, a new model of governance is needed in health centres, which coincided with both visitors and which is reflected in Sergas' document principals. We have a great experience in the educational system, where the autonomy of the centres has given rise to leadership teams and solid educational projects. We also want to introduce that management autonomy to health centers. Chiefs and service coordinators will be selected through competitions that value a local health plan project with a community medicine approach. This requires, in time, a rearrangement of functions between the primary staff that includes various aspects in which we are working: the increase in the solvency capacity of the nursing staff — for which we have already begun the processing of the decree that will allow them to renew, adjust or terminate the prescriptions of their optional companions — the promotion of the role of primary physiotherapy in functions such as dependency assessments or orthothesic material reports, the impulse of health training of general service staff — which will allow them to be enabled for tasks that can be released by the health care staff. And we also find it interesting to think in that committee of study about progress in better integration of CAP personnel into the rest of the primary. Of course, we're talking not only about reorganization but also about resource increases. The OPE we convened this year 2020 includes more than 140 primary care squares. In the formative offer we cover 100 percent of the accredited primary medicine squares. And also the 2021 budgets include the creation of 161 new squares for this level of assistance. In addition to human resources, we also plan to make a bet on the provision of the best technological resources, particularly in the development of the chronicity strategy. The Telea system, which was so useful during the pandemic, will be strengthened to connect to it all monitoring devices of patients, such as cardioimplantable devices — pacemakers, INR meters, insulin pumps or glucose meters. The generated information will be downloaded directly into the patient's electronic clinical history and, of course, will be available to all health personnel involved in the management of that patient. The electronic clinical history system itself will also evolve towards a process-oriented model called HCEPRO, which is being installed at primary care medical and nursing stations and for which specific modules will be designed for other categories such as pharmacy, physiotherapy or social work. With regard to specific investments in physical infrastructure, such as new health centres and improvement reforms or actions, let me take a closer look at those already planned for next week's budget showing. In any case, I tell you that, beyond the projects already under way, Sergas will develop a primary health-care infrastructure plan in which it will set new priorities according to existing needs. Let me also welcome the detailed explanation of the development of the Mental Health Plan, because, as the Health Committee of this Parliament has requested, it will be the subject of a specific appearance in this committee at the next few dates. In any case, I want to point out that mental health treatment will be a priority for this legislation, through this plan and also through the suicide prevention plan. The latter, which is already in progress, will be strengthened closely by the creation of records in the health areas through which professionals can better monitor and control patients who have experienced some attempt. Also in this legislation, we will put in place the plan for dementia. It will allow further progress to improve attention to neurodegenerative diseases initiated in the previous legislation with the Greek Ictus Care Plan and the integrated care processes of multiple sclerosis and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Precisely these integrated care processes, and in general mental health, are examples of a collaboration between primary care and hospital care that we want to enhance in the coming years. A new regulatory decree for the category of medical admission and clinical documentation is about to be adopted — in full trade union negotiations — in which a special reference will be made to its work as a link between health centres to facilitate communication and coordination between levels. We will also improve the post-hospital care that takes place from primary care, updating the Connecta 72 system, which enhances follow-up from primary patients' disease in their first days after discharge; and also involves primary pharmaceutical personnel in reconciling the medication of patients who receive this discharge. And, of course, just as it had been compromised in the primary care plan, we'll continue to expand the catalog of diagnostic tests accessible from primary care, increasing its resolution capacity and avoiding the patient's displacements and expectations. Another plan that will ensure this care continuity will be the future Greek Plan of Rare Diseases. We accept the unanimous demand of this Parliament to create multidisciplinary functional units for these patients and we will frame it in a roadmap that will continue the progress that in the previous legislation have brought about the creation of the register and the commission of rare diseases. The same will be true of the future Greek Oncohematology Plan, a process also initiated in the previous legislation and to which this Parliament has recently called for the development of a CAR-T therapy centre from the public health system as well. We have assumed this commitment and there will be an advanced cellular therapy strategy within the public health system in Galicia. But we also want this Italian Plan of Oncohematology to enhance early diagnosis and axillize the itinerary of clinical visits, implementing common care circuits and homogenizing more innovative and non-pharmacological therapies. Another of the compromised plans is the Hado plan. Experience shows that where home hospitalization is implanted becomes a success. It's one of the services most valued by citizenship. That's why we assume all the commitments to endowment of the plan squares and maintain the goal of extending this service to 100 percent of the citizenship of Galicia in this legislation. In fact, in the coming weeks, we'll end the processing of a decree that will create the professional category of "home hospitalization" because we're convinced of the projection that this service will have in the coming years. The Telea system discussed above will also increase home care from the hospital, as we will open the possibility of using it for the pharmacological follow-up of oncotoxic processes, monitoring of inflammatory bowel disease patients or cardiac rehabilitation patients, by setting an example. But beyond that home hospitalization, which will be used whenever possible, we must also improve patient care in our hospital centers in various ways. On the one hand, in accessibility. During the covid-19 crisis, the deadlines for those priority 1 patients and all those pathologies whose pathologies were covered by maximum time regulation were guaranteed. And we have two commitments for this legislation: to extend these maximum times to more pathologies and to create more rapid pathways and to put in place a plan to recover interventions, consultations and delayed tests, to return Galicia to where it was before the pandemic, with the best waiting lists of the historical series and below half the national average. We also want to work in security by reducing the risk of infections. Last year, the Committee on Control of Infections Related to Health Care was set up and we have several prevention programmes in collaboration with the Ministry and our own others, such as the Zero Denutrition Programme, for critical patients, or the InNO-CBR application, which helps prevent our social infections. We also want to work in quality by fighting pain. Sergas has made great progress a decade ago with the start of the first strategy of integral pain care. But today it is necessary to review the procedure of evaluating acute pain in the hospital and to develop a specific procedure for the approach of child pain. We are also going to work on humanization — or continue to work —, moving forward in the strategy already formulated before the pandemic and extending to all of the hospital centers measures such as the opening of pediatric ICUs and newborns twenty-four hours — to the progenitors, or the ICUs of adults as soon as possible. And, of course, with improvements in the humanization of infrastructures. All new construction or hospital expansion works of Sergas will take into account needs such as specific and adapted waiting areas in ICUs, acoustic isolations, nursing rooms, signage suitable for people with different types of disability or care adapted to ostomized people. I will not now list all the planned works in hospitals, which will be commented in more detail on the above-mentioned attendance of budgets, but I would like to point out that these works have common priorities, in line with those mentioned above: improving emergency and critical services, expanding hospital units for greater comfort of the people entering and their families, and empowering the day-to-day hospitals for this improvement of patient autonomy and also of their close relatives. I would like to point out that in the budgets for 2021, we are looking at the provision for important projects of improvement in almost all the comarcas hospitals. We have also projected for this legislation these performances, a bet, therefore, for the comarcial hospitals that are not left in the works. Also in the last public job offer, we will have a specific score for the provision of services in difficult-covered comarcal hospitals; an action that will, already advanced, be accompanied by a package of measures to encourage clinical practice in these hospitals and ensure their future viability — moving forward in the model that we shield with the Health District Creation Act. In general, staff coverage is a matter that concerns us and deals with us. We continue to demand, with little reception from the Government of Spain, the elimination of the limitation of the replacement rates, which prevents us from covering all the vacant squares we want. We insist on demanding the measures agreed by the International Health Council in April 2018 to speed up the creation of training squares in deficit specialities. As I have done before, Galicia has covered 100 percent of the markets offered in both family medicine and pediatrics, but it's not enough. So, beyond statements to the Ministry, we will do what is in our hand to ensure that training capacity of new professionals. In this legislation we will adopt a decree for the management of Galician specialized health training system and we will also take measures to encourage those professionals who collaborate as tutors in training rotations and in student practices. In fact, we will establish teacher accreditation as a requirement in transfer contests to accredited centres. We therefore work to have a training structure prepared to assume that higher level of accreditation of teaching units that we claim. And we are also interested in the stable coverage of these squares, so we insist on the OPE, so we insist on creating squares and that is why we bet on models of continuity contracts — with 122 professionals already hired and with the extension of this model in the contingency plan I mentioned earlier —. However, we are aware that there is there, that there is more personal without fixed place than it would be recommended, and, consequently, we will also advance in this legislation in the adoption of a new human resources reorganization plan for Sergas; a plan that would, of course, be easier to make if the Government freed us from that limitation of the rest rate, which it commented on earlier, and we could do that management on the whole of our vacant squares. After reviewing the situation and prospects for the pandemic, primary care, hospital care and human resources policy, I want to stop on two proposals for the future that will be priority in this legislation and determining for the future of Galician health: Community health and innovation. These are two aspects in which the pandemic shows that we have to invest in the future: a greater focus on health promotion and disease prevention, with greater momentum to research for innovative treatments, and on innovation itself. In the above-mentioned approach to primary care, this role of community medicine and the health centre is highlighted as an agent for improving local health. We also have examples in Galicia of programs to reduce sedentaryism and combat obesity, which worked locally with a health leader. We want to move on that path by involving all social agents in health improvement or healthy food programs. Technology can also help us with tools that enable citizens to record information about their progress in this kind of program in a personal folder that your doctor and nurse can access from clinical history. The same is true of socio-sanitary programs. We are working with the Social Policy Council in the new model of residences that must ensure constant medical and health care for residents, for which telemedicine can and should be very useful. This community-based vocation of health is also aimed at ensuring active aging. Galicia has achieved European recognition as a reference region in innovation in this area thanks to innovative public procurement programmes, such as Code 100, which are facilitating independent life and the social welfare follow-up of our biggest. And, with regard to minors, we will advance the development of the Xanela Aberta program, a portal where we want pediatricians and families to interact in the follow-up and promotion of healthy habits from pregnancy to adolescence. We'll also advance in prevention. We will continue with the screening and early detection programs of various types of cancer already known — those of breast and colorectal — and we are now extending it to Cervix. And we'll act on the problems of addiction in youth. In this legislation we will bring to this Parliament a new law on the prevention of children's addictions. This rule will renew the Alcohol Prevention Act in minors — in 2010 — and incorporate prevention measures for other addictions between young people; in particular, smoking, but also all those non-substantial addictions, such as those related to technology. As far as research is concerned, we want to work on the development of the research career and the regulatory framework for its recruitment. We will continue to advance in the integrated clinical research system that was created last year, which will allow better collaboration between centers and share research data. We will guide these research through projects such as living-labs, the Innovation Project or the Microbiology 4.0 project, and others that will allow us to investigate, and also transfer the results of this research to society and patients, following the mechanisms created in the past legislation through a pioneering decree on the valorization and transfer of research results. Finally, we want to continue to disseminate all the innovative experiences carried out by the different health departments. The Health Innovation Awards have been created, of which we will soon convene the second edition, we have a functioning health innovation platform, and next year we will launch the Xare Knowledge Management Platform. With it we will achieve greater dissemination of training and share the knowledge in health that will cherish the excellent professionals of our public system. I don't want to forget the role of one of our gems in research, the Galaga Foundation of Xenomic Medicine, an institution that we want to enhance electronically integrating its systems with those of Sergas, to facilitate the request for evidence and achieve its accreditation under the ISO Standard of quality clinical laboratories. The role of the health research institutes of the Coruña, Santiago and Vigo must also be highlighted in this issue. At this point, and at the end of the review by the general lines, I wanted to make a brief mention of the concrete actions provided by Health to fulfill the great commitments of the Board in this legislation. Health will cooperate with the ecological commitment. We have planned important actions in energy efficiency in our centres. And we'll participate, as it does not!, in the strategy of the circular economy of the Union in relation to health waste. Health will also collaborate with the demographic commitment. We've just discussed the beginning of the process of a very important law for Galicia, and from our department we're determined to contribute to this challenge by boosting fertility treatments in the public system. Health will also be in the attention of diversity. In past legislation a reference unit was created for transsexual minors, but we have to complete it with cross-sectional actions on the system. That's why we want to put in place the protocol on gender identity that guarantees equal and non-discriminatory treatment of people who support public health and ensure that our staff have the necessary training and awareness-raising to assist them. Last commitment: equality. Our human resources department is working with trade union organizations in the development of the first equality plan for Sergas staff. We also want to continue to make progress in offering the best treatments available to the Greeks and the Greeks, and with the best trained professionals, through clinical trials developed by health professionals and with the support of the three institutes that I had previously commented on, but also through the latest technologies: the ECMO, robotic surgery or the latest advances in oncology. We want to continue progressing also in multidisciplinary and integrated treatment, from primary care to tumor committees, with all the specialties of opinion and, of course, with the patients themselves making the clinical decisions — following a model as recognized in our health system as that of the breast units installed and operating in all public hospitals—. And today, on the eve of the Day Against Gender Violence, I also want to reaffirm Sergas' commitment to this fight. Next week we will present, together with the Employment and Equality Council, a new procedure that we will implement in the health system to contribute to the early detection of gender-based violence situations and help women get out of them. Before I finish, I want to make a special mention of the situation that is taking place these days at the Summer Hospital in connection with one of your professionals. First of all, it must be noted that the situation in the neighborhood of the Hospital de Verín is normalized and everything works properly. The Galician Health Service continues to count on all the professionals who perform their work there and recognize, as it could not be otherwise, their work. On the other hand, about Dr. Castrillo — as I have known since my stage in the health care area of Ourense — there is an open administrative process resulting from a complaint by another specialist — in this case a pediatrician — who considered the treatment this professional gave him in front of a family. Sergas has, for this purpose, independent inspection mechanisms, and contrasted in more than twenty-five years of history, which strictly, method and guarantees resolve these situations. In this sense, this process began in December last year, the corresponding documentation was collected and interviews were conducted with all parties. In this context, the Inspectorate now transfers its report on 5 November and sends it strictly to the usual administrative process. Dr. Castrillo may at his own time make all the allegations he considers appropriate. The process provides all guarantees to the parties. It is administrative and does not affect or will affect the service given to the population. I end by thanking all the people who every day, especially at such a difficult time as this, work to make a better public health from our public system. If I can talk about these advances today, it's because we grow up on a solid basis, in whose construction many professionals have left the best of themselves. In particular, I want to have a reminder for the work of all the people who accompanied me in the honor of being in front of this board.
