ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u161	Thank you, President. Good afternoon, everyone. The Socialist Parliamentary Group today brings forward a proposal which deals with the labour market situation of our young people and girls, urging the Government of Greece to immediately put in place a youth employment shock plan that designs measures that contribute to the establishment of a quality employment framework, combating the despondent effect of young people and young women in Greece and facilitating their work integration. We will make a preliminary diagnosis of the situation of the conditions under which we are based on data from the third quarter of the EPA and a report prepared by the UGT on this subject. The data, as you will see, qualify the situation of the labour market for youth in Galicia as precarious, unstable and without rights. Precautionary is a set of situations that determine a situation of disadvantage or inequality, characterized in this case by a short duration of contracts. Most contracts with young people and girls have a duration of less than one year, as we will see, with high rates of temporality, reductions in unwanted days, high index of part-time days — as we will see — the generational gap, lower wages compared to the rest of the employed, excess overtime with or without pay — almost always compulsory realization — and all that entails greater, even a system of danger to the health of workers, young people and girls. Well, we've been witnesses in Galicia for a while that these variables perfectly define the situation in the German labour market. The third quarter EPA data reflect the alarming reduction of the active population. We have 17,400 more inactive boys and girls, 7,600 more unemployed girls and girls and a loss of active population, as I said, of more than 20,000 people in this collective. If we compare the evolution of the unemployment situation between the years 2019 and 2020 — data, as I said, today — we can see how a significant increase of unemployed people under 35 years of age takes place in absolute terms of 7,600. By age, within the youth group, what presents a greater number of people in unemployment is that of boys and girls from 25 to 29:22,100, nothing more and nothing less. But, as I said, even more serious is that the evolution of unemployment results in the fall of activity and the increase in inactive population, because, in addition to analysing the population in a situation of unemployment — as we have done — we take into account the evaluation of the active population, we see a strong fall: 20,200 people less among young people and girls. As far as the occupied population is concerned, there are currently 208,900 persons under 35 in Galicia in this situation, which is a contraction over a year ago of 27,900. But also very worrisome is the rate of unemployment of young people in Greece, which stands at 21.6%; 9.8 points higher than that of the age group, which is 11.8%. In just one year, young people in Greece lose four points of activity rate. In sectors, the expulsion of the labour market, you know what happens mainly in the service sector, which is where our boys and girls are represented most, in the hospitality sector, for example, and also in the business sector. The high level of unemployment and the poor quality of unemployment are the main lines that define the precarious employment of youth. The employment policies that have been developed by the Galician Union, the active employment policies that compete exclusively for this autonomous community, are transferred from the state to the community and have been totally disabled for them. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u178	Thank you, President. Good afternoon, ladies and gentlemen. The current economic crisis has its origin in a health crisis — as everyone knows — and the consequences on the lives and health of people around the world are potentially devastating. The economic consequences are a direct result of the strategy of minimizing the human costs of the epidemic in conditions of collective uncertainty. The strategy to eliminate the epidemic has significantly reduced economic activity. Production and consumption in normal conditions are inseparable from social contact; the longer the deepest and most incalculable social isolation lasts are the consequences for individuals and companies. The evolution of the economy is very determined by health evolution and, therefore, by the possibility of doing social activities normally, among them to move easily. No one has complete security, because no one knows what's going to happen. We don't know the duration of the crisis, whether there's going to be bigger or how, if, on the contrary, research efforts are making possible a vaccine or two, according to the latest news. We have to bet more and more on research, development and innovation. It is essential to move forward towards new ways of action in industrial policy, especially in the current context of growing fragility of the international economy. The expectations for the current year and the years 2021 and 2022 speak of a slowing of the very important growth due to the pandemic of the coward-19. The emergence of new activities and new forms of production obliges the public to strengthen this structural transformation. The central element of this new stage of industrial production is knowledge, which would have to lead our economy towards greater productivity, which in turn will allow for mobility in employment. It is essential to improve the efficiency of R & D+i policies, to support entrepreneurship and to stimulate organizational change and technological and infrastructure modernization. It is essential to make greater economic effort, but it must be accompanied by the objectives of minimizing barriers to growth and improving the productivity of the industrial fabric; not only of the most intensive industrial tissues in knowledge, but also of the traditional industrial sectors. It is necessary to establish a realistic and visionary industrial policy that will allow Galicia to have the opportunity to win its future on the 4.0 industry stage. The Galicia Innova 2020 Plan of the Galician Union had as its objective to consolidate Galicia as an innovative territory to improve the competitiveness of businesses, promote sustainable growth and generate quality jobs. In order to achieve these objectives, the plan aimed to: increase staff employed in R & D activities by 11%; increase the number of businesses with innovative activities by 15 %; add 20 % more to business spending in innovative activities; increase business volume by 15 % in new or improved products; and achieve 1.7 % gross domestic product expenditure. Or 1,7 %! "Then we'll see that nothing further from reality." The Galicia Innova 2020 Plan of the Galician Union failed to meet these objectives, which is insufficient and insufficient to meet the challenges of the sector in the face of this pandemic caused by the cover-19. Research, development and innovation must be a strategic sector for the public policies of the Galician Union in the face of this health and economic crisis that we are living in. The Socialist Parliamentary Group calls on the Galician Union to bet on a strengthened and results-oriented R & D, giving priority to activities with a common knowledge base of some intensity, strengthening the science and technology system and increasing public contributions to it. Our group also bets to put in place mechanisms that encourage private participation in the general innovation system, so its spending advances towards 1.5% of gross domestic product. There is one axiom that is always fulfilled: it only increases private investment in R+D+i when it previously increases sustainably in time public investment. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2020-11-24-DSPG013.u180	Good afternoon. Far from betting on the knowledge economy, the People's Party has cut public investment in research over the last decade, making Galicia not only move forward but lose positions. The German investment in research, development and innovation in relation to its GDP fell from 1.04 to 0.94 of the years 2017 and 2018 and went back in 2019 to 0.93%. Just 0.93% compared to 1.24% average in the Spanish state, occupying the eighth position behind, for example, Euskadi, which takes its investment in R & D+i to 1.96% of its GDP — more than twice as much as Galicia — or Navarra, with 1.68%; Catalonia, with 1.52%; or Castile-Leon, with 1.3% of its GDP, 30% more than Galicia in relative terms. There is still 2.2% of the EU average. And it's also a long way from 1.53% that is invested in the Northern Region of Portugal and is gaining positions in the EU while Galicia loses them. These facts denote, ladies and gentlemen, a tendency; it's not a fixed picture or a circumstantial question. Galicia loses positions in the EU parameters, given that it accurately describes the zero degree of commitment to the R & D+i of the People's Party's governments, while the territories of our closest surroundings, which we must necessarily compare — as we insist, northern Portugal or Basquedi — can move forward and gain positions. Today we occupy position 212 of the 238 European regions analysed through the EU parameters that measure the investment made in R & D+i. The causes of this regression have all their origin in the poor allocation of economic resources, although they are manifested in different ways that we are not going to fully detach today, but we are going to put an end only to one of the most important — not to say the most important — human resources management. All researchers agree that the lack of job stability is the main obstacle to the progress of research itself. The need to be constantly concerned about continuity at work; the need to be involved in procedures and deadlines for renewing grants; the fact that contracts have a shorter duration than required in order to gain access to certain investigative status and thus to larger projects; the fact that there are public structures where 90% of investigator staff are possible; low wages, etc. They are big traps from the point of view of consolidating the system, because these difficulties cause abandonment or escape; that is, a stay in a foreign country that should be temporary may ultimately become due to the better conditions compared to what they find. In any of the assumptions the system loses very well-prepared people who have invested a lot of public money, which will, at best, produce knowledge and wealth in other territories. The recruiting system of the investigator's staff is so precarious that it has to be self-stretched to keep going. Thus, we find standards that speak of the expectation of overcoming a public procurement process, or of the contractor's commitment to the continuity of the investigator as requirements for opting for certain projects. All to draw the possibility — very real — that the investigator in question has no contract in force at the time of the proceedings. This reality leads to the best option — if not the only one — to gain that necessary stability is to become a personal researcher; a situation in which, although stable from the working point of view, they cannot be devoted to full-time research either. And that whenever that possibility exists in the particular area of research. Other circumstances that require the attention of the investigator's staff are administrative, technical and management work and the evaluation continues. The lack of properly trained and specialized management personnel not only causes a great workload for the researcher's staff, but also causes ineffectiveness in resource management; just as a system of evaluation continues to be bureaucratic and redundant in excess, it does not respond to its primary purpose either. And, as always, precariousness and eventuality penalize women above all that, despite being more than 50 percent of the people licensed and having better academic records, they get to lower management positions. So we have abandonment, escape, complementary teaching, management and coordination, lack of measures to promote equality and conciliation, low wages, etc. Circumstances which together lead — as they have been warning from certain areas — in which we have not guaranteed even the necessary generational relief. The view of the set, as I said, that these facts do not give to the convenience. We'll never get to weave an efficient knowledge management network in these conditions. It is necessary to take action, to start by increasing investment in R+D+i and by profoundly modifying the model of recruitment and management of human resources throughout the Welsh research system. In this regard, the representative of InvesteGal, in his presence in the Reactivation Commission, left some reflections of interest that we made ourselves and also some proposals. First reflection: human resources are precisely the cornerstone of research, so we must value the starting point by professionalizing and stabilizing the investigator's staff. And second reflection: the financing of research is not a expense, but an investment with correct yields in the medium and long term. And he added four proposals: giving stability, eliminating dead periods caused by grant calendars, hiring management staff and implementing measures to promote equality and reconciliation. At the BNG we understand that the proper consideration of research as a strategic sector — but the fact that the success we can have in this area depends on improving the conditions in which the researcher's staff develops their activities — obliges us to provide the sector with a genuine career of the researcher's staff who clearly contemplates all their phases and guarantees decent working conditions. As for our amendment to addendum on the post-doctoral call, as you will know, this is the only call in Galicia for those doctors to continue their research career. A modality we don't agree with since BNG and with which we are immensely critical of their conditions, but which is practically the only option today if our researchers and researchers want to stay in Galicia and continue their professional career here. In our autonomous community, the execution of health research is structured — as has been said here — around three biomedical research institutes: the Santiago Institute of Health Research (IDIS), the Institute of Biomedical Research (INIBIC) and the Institute of Health Research (IISGS). The research groups of these three major institutes make it possible for them to carry out most of the health research actions in Galicia, which have their center in public hospitals. And it's important to note that, at the same time, these researchers from the institutes achieve synergies with researchers from the Greek universities. Therefore, the provision and budget of health research are mainly found in these centres. In Galicia we have two institutes that were accredited by the Carlos III Institute of Health. In particular, the Santiago Institute, in the year 2010 – also believed in 2015 – and that of the Coruña, in 2014. He's working on Vigo's accreditation right now. This is an important element, since official accreditation granted requires confirmation of the existence of researched basic and clinical groups, as well as the existence of an adequate infrastructure to support this research. In Galicia there are over 150 research groups around these three institutes, which are structured in different areas: oncology, genetics and systems biology, endocrinology, neuroscience and epidemiology and public health. These groups carry with them a network that includes about 1,500 researchers, with exclusive or shared dedication to the clinic, in addition to staff and support staff. Despite economic difficulties, in recent years the German research institutes have significantly increased their sources of funding. In 2009, the revenue they collected was the fourth part of what is currently being obtained, which clearly indicates the momentum that biohealth research is having in Galicia. But this isn't really important. Of course, the quality of researchers from these research institutes is the reason why this increase in fund-raising was allowed, but the fundamental thing is that the publication of scientific articles has multiplied, with a greater impact factor; in recent years the number of clinical trials has doubled and observational studies have been quintupled; and essentially, steps have been taken to transfer research results to citizens, with already significant number of patents obtained and licensed. With regard to collective agreements, seven health research foundations existed in Galicia in 2009, and none of them formalized any collective agreement with their workers. The first thing we've been working on was to recast and unify that dispersal to group it up into the three research foundations that exist right now, to strengthen structures and take advantage of synergies with universities and research institutes. Right now, for the first time since these research foundations exist, they are negotiating specific collective agreements for each of them. The counselor said it, precisely this morning, in his intervention. You, Mrs Prado, indicate in the text of the GNP that there have been researchers working for more than 20 years on temporary contracts. It's true, Mrs. Prado. It's true this recognition. And it is also true what the spokesman of the Socialist Group said here. All the governments are engaged in this. But I repeat again that for the first time since they exist, they are working on an agreement. In any case, it is wrong to identify the existence of a collective agreement with which these working conditions are worthy. There are a lot of entities and companies that don't have their own collective agreement, and that's not why workers are mistreated, and they have working conditions that are appropriate. On the other hand, you also say in the GNP: "Let a work agreement be closed in which pay conditions must be considered to be based at least on the proposal made by the committees." This does not seem to be moderately reasonable, and not because of which the conditions proposed by the committees — I do not come in or out of what the committee is going to propose — but because this is not a negotiation, it seems better to impose an agreement. It is therefore unreasonable for you, on the one hand, to propose that a negotiating table be held and, on the other hand, that criteria only be taken from one side to negotiate. There's no need to negotiate for that. I'll tell you again that for the first time you're working on the goal of formalizing collective agreements in these entities and that different important aspects are being elaborated on the rights and duties of workers; among others, the work schedule, the schedule, vacations, the permit regime, the surpluses, the representation rights... we believe that you should continue to work on it, but logically, through negotiation, because it's the way in which these agreements are established, and not through taxation, which seems to be what you propose. Nothing else and thank you very much.
